Chapter 19: Flowers and flour

Chapter Summary

And strangely, it felt like it would turn out just fine. She could do this. If it all unfolded the way she hoped, it’d be more than worth it.

And maybe, just maybe, she could make her little Nicky proud.

Chapter Notes

didn't take me three months this time :')
but that also means i didn’t take a lot of time to proofread so mind ur business pls

See the end of the chapter for more notes

“So, you and Rio seemed… cozy.” Wanda’s teasing sounded through the cafe, her elbows resting on the counter while Agatha poured her coffee order into a mug. It was still early morning, too early for the barista to have to deal with her best friend’s commentary on her (no longer?) lack of love life.

Luckily, they were the only ones in the cafe that morning. Luckily, because it meant that nobody could see the way Agatha’s cheeks burned from thinking about her and Rio. Also, because it meant that there would be no witnesses if she killed Wanda for poking the formerly emotionally unavailable bear too hard.

Deflecting, as usual and mostly out of habit, Agatha scoffed. “You got that from, what, a five-minute interaction?”

The redhead smirked. “Can’t help that you make it that obvious. Your eyes practically turn into hearts whenever you look at her. The same goes for her.”

Agatha pretended like it didn’t affect her. As if the thought of Rio looking at her like she hung the moon and stars in the sky didn’t make her stomach flip. Sometimes she wished she could’ve had a third-person perspective just to see the way the florist looked at her whenever Agatha wasn’t paying attention.

The cafe owner figured it wouldn’t be too far off from the way her own eyes softened whenever they ran along the smooth edges of Rio’s face, carefully watching the way her eyes lit up when she showed off that real, tooth-gapped smile.

She had thought it before, but Agatha was thinking it again: she was so fucking screwed.

Wordlessly, Agatha served her best friend a piece of freshly baked pecan caramel cake, after only a short second of wondering whether she still deserved it after all of her teasing. Now that she was already in for it anyway, she decided it might be a good time to update the redhead about everything that had transpired between her and Rio.

While Agatha recounted the details of their trip to New York, Wanda purposely shifted her gaze from her coffee to her cake, to Agatha and back, cautious enough not to make her best friend feel ‘perceived too much’, whatever she had meant by that.

Still, Wanda listened closely to Agatha’s story, paying attention to the words she chose and the tone of her voice whenever she spoke about Rio. It wasn’t hard for her to see that her friend had walked herself quite deep into the ocean of romantic feelings. She hoped that Agatha had finally learned how to swim, and otherwise, Wanda was standing right by her side, ready to throw her a lifeline.

Eager to find out just how necessary that lifeline would be, Wanda started poking. “So, what’s stopping you?”

Agatha finished pouring the frothed milk over her espresso and looked up, her face carrying a look of genuine confusion. “What do you mean? Stopping me from what?”

“From being girlfriends,” she replied, her voice casual as she raised her shoulders in a small shrug. “You already act like it.”

“I—” Agatha started, then closed her mouth again, not sure what to say to that. She slowly put away the empty milk jug, her eyebrows knitted together in the slightest frown, undoubtedly asking herself the same question. She didn’t know what was holding her back. They did kind of act like it. And Agatha surely spent most of her free—and not-so-free—time thinking about the florist. There hadn’t been anyone in a long time who preoccupied Agatha’s mind as much as Rio had. And she had stopped pushing those thoughts away a while ago.

“Are you waiting for her to ask?” Wanda’s pause after the question was intentional, closely reading the expression written over the cafe owner’s face. She saw the way Agatha halted her movements, quite literally stopping to think about it. Before she could conclude that, yes, maybe she was waiting for Rio to ask, the other woman interrupted her thoughts, ready to come in swinging with some rationality. “Because after everything you told me about her, I don’t think she would. Not unless she was 1000% sure that you’d say yes.”

“Why would she think I’d say no?”

Agatha raked her brain for every interaction she’d had with Rio lately, and couldn’t think of a reason why the younger woman would think Agatha wasn’t interested. She had shown up for her when it mattered; she allowed Rio to show up for her, too. She kissed her. Texted her. Invited her over after their trip for just a few minutes of extra time. She hadn’t run away, not like she usually would. How much clearer could she make her intentions?

“You’re not exactly an open book, Harkness,” her friend said with a pointed look, a single eyebrow raised, daring Agatha to claim otherwise.

Regardless, the older woman stuck to her first and foremost argument. “I’ve never done anything to make her think I’m not interested.”

She lifted the cup to her lips, taking a sip of her coffee. Agatha didn’t intend to be stubborn; she just didn’t see an issue. For once, she had been handling things rather normally, she thought to herself. Granted, it might’ve taken a few pushes from both her son and Wanda, but she’d managed to handle her situation with Rio better than she had in… years.

“I know they say actions speak louder than words, Agatha, but I feel like Rio might still appreciate the verbal confirmation.” Wanda could only hope this knowledge would not scare off her friend, pushing her deeper into the water while a storm was brewing.

Agatha realised that her friend’s advice might hold some truth. The Rio she knew was affected deeply by her past relationships, and oftentimes worried about being too much, moving too fast, or reading into things that weren’t there. The florist had opened up to her for a reason. Thus, Agatha felt like she should probably deal with that information more consciously, more deliberately.

“Yeah, okay.” Agatha gave a small nod. She wasn’t sure if that was to Wanda or to herself, but it didn’t matter. “Maybe I should ask her? Is that weird?”

Although Wanda was happy with the progress they had made, she still gave Agatha an incredulous look. “Why on earth would that be weird?”

Her voice softened significantly, even more so than the redhead had expected. “I don’t know. I don’t really do… this.’’ Agatha fidgeted with the small handle of her cup that was resting on the counter, twirling her finger around it. “I don’t know how to.”

From the corner of her eye, she caught Wanda’s movements, but didn’t register it fully until she felt a warm, gentle hand cover her own. “You’re doing better than you think. Stop overthinking it.”

The reassurance circled around her, wrapping up in a warm hug and giving her a tight squeeze, just to tell her that everything would be okay. And strangely, it felt like it would turn out just fine. She could do this. If it all unfolded the way she hoped, it’d be more than worth it.

And maybe, just maybe, she could make her little Nicky proud.

—————

Across the street, Rio was setting up her shop, getting it ready for another week. She had received fresh flowers earlier that morning. The florist carefully trimmed the stems and dunked them in clean water, before putting them in water-filled buckets and scattering them throughout the store.

If anyone thought to notice that there had been more purple in this delivery, that was not done deliberately.

It was purely a coincidence.

And it surely had nothing to do with the fact that she could not stop thinking about the neighbouring cafe across the street.

At least, that was what Rio told herself while she reached for the lavender bunches. The scents swirled around her like a balm, soft and clean. It was soothing, reminding her of the spray on her nightstand that she would mist over her pillows and blankets whenever she couldn’t sleep due to stress.

Not only the purple reminded her of the older woman, though. She looked at the blush pink garden roses, their delicate edges curling like shy secrets. Rio cradled one gently, running her fingers through the petals as if she were tucking her own secrets in between, then folding them over each other like the lid of a big, wooden chest, locking it tightly.

Her phone buzzed now: only a notification from an order coming in. She didn’t open it, but the sound yanked her from her daydream.

She shook her head and moved on to the Anemones, setting a bundle of mixed colours into a clear vase, watching as the petals bobbed slightly in the water. She thought about their various meanings—anticipation being the one that stood out to her the most; the anticipation of the beauty of spring, the anticipation of good things to come. Rio held out hope that things would take a turn for the better.

She much preferred this meaning over the one that represented forsaken love. The florist refused to believe that that was the sign the universe was giving her.

Her eyes drifted to the clock. It was almost time for her break. Rio’s stomach flipped, not with hunger, but with nerves. Anticipation.

Because her break meant going to the cafe. Going to the cafe meant seeing Agatha. Seeing Agatha meant getting that funny feeling in her chest and stomach, and her cheeks hurting from smiling so much.

Only… they hadn’t really talked about New York. Not properly. And over the weekend, the older woman had been quieter, occupied with Nicky. Which made sense. Total sense. Rio told herself that it made sense. Repeatedly, actually.

Though a part of her—the annoying, overthinking, irrational, negative part of her—worried that maybe Agatha was pulling away. Maybe the night in the hotel room had changed the pace, and not in the way Rio had hoped.

Should she have insisted on staying in separate rooms?

The florist didn’t have it in her to regret it. If she could go back in time, she certainly wouldn’t change it.

Maybe I should give her space, she thought, twiddling with the label of a flower food packet that had fallen from the counter. Just for a little while. Let her reach out first.

Don’t overwhelm her.

Don’t be too much.

Please, Rio, for the love of the gods, don’t be too much. Not again.

Her brown eyes flicked to the backroom, momentarily considering to turn on the coffee maker she kept there.

But then again… wouldn’t it be weird not to go?

Skipping out on her coffee run would say more than showing up ever could. Wouldn’t it make it seem like something was wrong?

It would break the rhythm, pull a thread from the tapestry of their almost-something, yet still not-really-anything, and unravel it quietly.

She exhaled, wiping her hands on her apron, simultaneously brushing some dirt off it. “It’s just coffee.”

If she purposely untied her hair to let it drape over her shoulders just the way Agatha liked it, that was entirely Rio’s business. She fluffed her hair and flipped it over to one side, breaking up the middle part.

When Rio took a deep breath and flipped the ‘Open’ sign to ‘Be right back :)’, the small smile on her face appeared automatically. It had been yet another gift handcrafted by Nicky, which she received soon after her visits to the cafe became more frequent. The little boy had drawn colourful flowers around the edges, and even added a small stick figure that was supposed to represent Rio. He was worried that ‘customers wouldn’t know where she went’ and ‘would buy flowers somewhere else’ or worse, ‘would be sad’. Clearly, he felt passionate about the flower shop, a sense of responsibility growing inside of him after all the botany lessons he received from the florist.

There were little traces of Agatha and Nicky scattered all throughout Rio’s life; in her shop, in her apartment, in the dearest chamber of her heart.

She could only hope that those traces would settle inside her core, rooting themselves deeply and blossoming into something more firm as time passed.

Upon pushing her way through the door leading into the cafe, Rio’s eyes quickly darted across the room, scanning the space for a sign of her favourite barista. The florist’s eyebrows drew together when she couldn’t see her, and only found Lilia tending to the few customers that were inside.

Was Agatha trying to avoid Rio so hard that she didn’t come to the cafe?

No, that’d be absurd. Right?

Although Rio felt somewhat like a kicked puppy, she schooled her expression into a more neutral one, even mustering a small smile to greet Lilia. It had been a while since she had caught up with the older woman anyway. Perhaps this was a good thing.

Besides, her pastries never failed to make Rio feel better. She swore they were heaven-sent.

Lilia pretended not to notice the way Rio’s face dropped when she entered and opted for a more casual approach instead.

They shared some small talk while Lilia prepared Rio’s coffee. Even though the florist ordered the exact same coffee that she would whenever Agatha was around, it tasted different. Maybe Lilia’s ratio of espresso, frothed milk and vanilla syrup was different. That was probably it. Because it’d be pathetic if Rio thought that anything created by Agatha just tasted better, right?

And Rio could never pathetically down bad for anyone. She certainly didn’t have a track record to show it.

With a bittersweet smile, the younger woman sipped her coffee, nodding along to Lilia’s life updates. She had taken a seat on the barstool off the side of the counter, her favourite spot to sit whenever she caught up with Agatha, or teased Billy about his boyfriend. That way, she didn’t stand in the way of any new customers, while still being close enough to talk to the person working the counter. Most of the time, that would be Agatha.

After a while, the burning question, the one she had put on hold from the moment she stepped inside, finally came out. Hopefully, as casually as possible. “Where’s Agatha?”

The older woman fought the smirk that was tempting to curl her lips, and pushed her grey curls out of her face, just to have something to do. Finally, Lilia thought.

“She’s not in today. Something about a meeting with her coffee supplier.” Lilia’s voice came out gentle, her gaze steady on Rio’s face to read any micro expression off of it.

Rio let out a soft hum, nodding her head once to signal her understanding. “Oh, okay,” was all she was able to respond.

Inside her head, the thoughts were racing past at a very fast pace, and Rio was trying her hardest to control them. To not let herself slip into the downward spiral that she tended to get sucked into.

Would she really avoid me?

That wouldn’t make sense, the quiet, timid voice of reason reminded her. She invited you over when you got back from New York, just to spend more time. Why bother if she was going to avoid you after?

Maybe she was just being polite, the irrational voice dominated, as it always did. Always so fucking loud.

Lilia seemingly noticed Rio’s inner turmoil and took pity on her, eager to pull her out of her thoughts and back into the present. “Anything I can help you with?”

As soon as the question was spoken out loud, the older woman could see the cogs turning in Rio’s head, and she could almost physically see the thoughts bouncing from one side to the other, contemplating whether she should confide in Lilia.

“I don’t know…” The words came out more like a sigh, heavy and loaded with the weight that was on Rio’s shoulders. She fidgeted with a wooden coffee stirrer, resisting the urge to snap it in half. With her head still down, she merely looked at Lilia through her lashes. “You don’t happen to have your tarot cards on you, do you?”

As if Lilia would ever go anywhere without her cards. Rio should know better. Still, the older woman didn’t correct her and spared her from the sassy response she would’ve gotten in any other scenario. Instead, her gaze softened. “Honey, I don’t need a reading to know that you’re in deep.”

The florist’s face flushed red, all the way to the tips of her ears and down her neck. She knew she had probably not been as subtle about it as she had hoped, but to hear the words spoken back to her like that was still making her feel embarrassed. Though she should’ve expected Lilia to know exactly what was going on, even if Rio had tried harder to keep it under the radar. Lilia always knew everything. It was kind of scary.

Rio bit her lip, worrying the bottom one between her teeth. Another deep breath. The small stick in her hands was closer to snapping. Her voice made her sound incredibly small when she spoke. “I’m not looking for clarity on my feelings…”

“Oh, my sweet Rio… I know she’s hard to read, but trust me, you’ll have your answers soon.” She placed her hand gently over the younger woman’s, caressing the back of it with her thumb. It felt comforting. Something about Lilia always felt comforting, like she somehow could make the worst situations feel much lighter and offer new perspectives whenever others had been stuck.

“Is that you talking, or the deck?”

Lilia cocked her head to the side. “Does it matter?”

“I guess not.” Rio’s smile was tight-lipped, not exactly reaching her eyes. She held onto Lilia’s words with a secure grip, careful not to let them get caught in the whirlwind that was taking place in her brain.

The florist had always had faith in Lilia, and the older woman hadn’t exactly been wrong before. Thus, she used that knowledge to reassure herself; she’d have answers soon. And hopefully, it’d be the answer she desired.

Her inner strife had not yet fully settled, but there wasn’t much she could do. Rio had to get back to work and somehow find a moment to talk to Agatha later. Time to put on her big-girl pants and confront her head-on.

But she’d give herself juuust a little bit of extra time. Not because she was worried. She was being so cool about it.

After finishing her coffee and taking the pastry that Lilia insisted she brought back, Rio returned to her workplace. At least there was enough work for her to remain distracted for the rest of the day, she realised as she looked around at all the flowers she still had to get ready for sale.

She was deeply focused on snipping the stems and removing leaves from the yellow coneflowers so they could be placed next to the daffodils. The colours in the shop were getting brighter as the weather turned nicer, and it was actually lightening Rio’s sombre mood.

The familiar jingle of the bell broke her concentration. When she looked up, she saw a young man walk in, wearing a friendly smile on his face. His demeanour seemed a bit nervous, like he wasn’t sure if he was at the right place.

Rio eyed him attentively as he smoothed the sleeves of his only slightly wrinkled button-up shirt. He clutched his phone in his other hand like it was his salvation.

The florist offered her usual warm smile, gathering the scraps of the stems she had cut and throwing them away, before brushing her hands off on her apron. “Hey there,” she said, gently. “Take your time.” She waited a while before addressing him again. “Just looking, or can I help with something specific?”

The young man hesitated. “Uh… yeah. Actually, I was hoping you could recreate a bouquet from a picture?”

He showed her the phone, holding it out like he was passing off something sacred. Rio took it and squinted at the screen.

The bouquet in the image was… elaborate, to say the least. At first glance, it was beautiful: lush and vibrant, with a romantic colour palette consisting of blushes, soft purples, and creamy whites, filled with rich green. But as her florist-trained eyes scanned over it, something prickled at her gut. There was something... off.

She recognised most of the flowers—garden roses as she had just restocked, peonies, lilacs—but some of them didn’t look quite real. Their shapes were just slightly… wrong. Too smooth, or too many layers. And some blooms didn’t belong together at all. Rio could swear she spotted a few hybrids she was almost certain didn’t exist. Not where they lived, at least.

“Where’d you find this?” she asked, curiosity colouring her voice.

The young man looked almost sheepish, rubbing the back of his neck. “Umm, online..”

“Do you know where it’s from?”

Rio never backed away from a challenge. If she could make this customer happy, she’d try her damn best. And if they were flowers that bloomed in other countries, she’d merely add that country to her list of places to visit.

She took another long, hard look at the screen. Her gut was still pulling her in each and every direction, until the final realisation hit her: the colours were too saturated, the way only something digitally created could be.

“Well, umm,” he stumbled, his embarrassment showing on his face in the shape of a bright red blush. “ChatGPT made it.”

Open the schools, please, the florist thought.

Wordlessly, he swiped at his screen, minimising the image to show the full chat.

Of course! Here’s a bouquet of flowers that represents the message you want to send to your girlfriend. Followed by the picture in question. And at last, it ended with, Would you like me to change anything — the colours, or adding your girlfriend’s favourite flower, for example?

“Oh, right.” Rio angled the phone a little, frowning, but trying to hide it at the same time. She zoomed in on one particular flower that looked like a cross between a camellia and a ranunculus, but with, what she thought was, impossible petal symmetry. “And the prompt was…?”

He sighed, and with a few more taps, scrolled to his inserted prompt. “I asked it for a bouquet that says ‘love, appreciation, and loyalty’. Thought I’d save myself the awkward guesswork.”

He wasted literal tons of water and energy for this?

God, we’re fucking doomed.

Rio snorted before she could help herself. She always tried to be professional, but this was getting ridiculous. Was she being pranked? “Well, it gave you something, alright.”

The customer blinked. “What—”

“Look,” Rio started, fighting the urge to rub her now throbbing temples, “I can make this for you, besides the obvious ones that don’t exist, but it won’t have the meaning you’re going for.”

“What do you mean?”

Rio handed the phone back and crossed her arms, nails digging into her own skin to keep her from lashing out. She was gentle-parenting herself through this one.

“That bouquet has a red camellia, which does mean love. But next to it, there’s yellow carnations; although beautiful, those mean rejection. The hydrangeas can be used for gratitude, frigidity or bad luck, depending on who you ask.” The florist watched as the long man’s colour slowly drained from his face, his eyebrows coming together in a small frown. “Oh, and that weird-looking one that is supposed to resemble a foxglove? That’s not even real. But even if it was a foxglove, it’d symbolise deceit.”

Rio was always open to learning new things, but in this case, she was certain that she was right. Her brow arched. “Not exactly the message you want to send, unless you’re trying to confuse the hell out of someone.”

At this point, the customer looked horrified. “Oh god.. I was gonna give this to my girlfriend for our anniversary.”

“So, uh,” Rio pressed her lips together to keep from laughing. “Good call double-checking. She might’ve dumped you out of pure symbolism.”

“I thought I was being clever. Thought this shit was supposed to make things easier.” He groaned and buried his face in his hands for a second.

“Yeah, no offence, but AI doesn’t know shit about flowers,” Rio said, her tone teasing but not unkind. “Just burns electricity to make some questionable pictures by stealing from others’ work. You want meaning? Ask the nerd who lives and breathes this stuff.” She patted the side of a metal bucket for emphasis. “That’d be me.”

Call her a nerd, call her a loser, but at least she fucking knew what she was talking about.

He looked up. “Okay… can you help me make something similar, but not, you know… offensive?”

“Absolutely.” She winked. “Your bouquet’s reputation and your relationship will be safe with me.”

They worked together for the next fifteen minutes, Rio pulling blooms from her shelves with precision and care. The young man visibly relaxed as the bouquet came together in her hands, breathing easier with every thoughtful addition and the explanation behind it.

When she finally handed him the finished piece, his eyes widened. “This is so much better!”

The florist gave a satisfied nod. “And this time, it says exactly what you wanted it to say.”

He paid, thanked her profusely, and left with the bouquet cradled like treasure. As the door fell shut behind him, Rio chuckled softly to herself. This was one of the most absurd things she had experienced at her job, and there was only one person she wanted to share it with. She knew Agatha had been busy, but she pulled out her phone and fired off a quick text regardless.

please never let nicky discover AI

need him to use his critical thinking skills

After her conversation with Lilia, she didn’t allow herself to overthink the action—she wanted to text Agatha, so she did.

Only a moment later, the typing dots blinked into life on her screen.

Agatha: Too late. He asked Siri yesterday if she could make his broccoli disappear.

Rio laughed, setting her phone aside with a grin. She returned to her flowers, heart light, mind already imagining what message she would’ve wanted to get across if she were creating a bouquet for Agatha. Both her mind and eyes immediately drifted to the deep red ranunculus on her left, after which her gaze fell on a small bunch of waxflowers.

Shaking her head lightly, she muttered to herself, “Too much, Rio. Too much.”

Though her heart swelled at the mental image of her handing the bouquet to Agatha, and she allowed herself to daydream about the words she’d choose to express her feelings. She didn’t feel nearly brave enough at the moment, but she felt like she was finally a step closer, even if it was another baby step.

—————

When Rio hadn’t seen Agatha all of Tuesday either, she was growing restless again. Not that her nerves had ever fully settled down, but at least she felt determined. After all, she had a plan.

The plan: stop being a fucking baby and just talk to her.

Easy enough, right?

It would’ve been easy, had she not been up to her neck in work. She hadn’t even had time for a proper break due to the uptake of online orders. On the weekend, Alice had helped her optimise her website, adding new tags and keywords in the coding so that it’d be easier to find Rio online. She had teamed up with a local delivery team, and now she could take orders from neighbouring zip codes.

It was good for business, but not so good for Rio’s psyche. She was tired.

“Good morning, welcome in—”

That same fatigue is what made her believe she was hallucinating when she looked up at the door. Instead of seeing her favourite Harkness (the jury was still out on which of the two exactly won that title), she saw her least favourite one. In the doorway stood Evanora, like a shadow that didn’t belong in the peaceful space the florist had curated, and Rio had to do a double-take.

She couldn’t remember the woman ever setting foot in her store before. Rio wouldn’t even have known what Evanora looked like if she hadn’t asked Agatha to show her, right after the phone call between mother and daughter that caused the cafe owner to suffer a panic attack.

The older woman’s posture was stiff, her expression like polished stone. Unreadable, cold, and unforgiving. Though Rio didn’t think the woman had ever smiled a real smile. She wore a deep plum coat, despite the spring warmth, and carried nothing but her tightly wound self-righteousness.

Rio’s hands stilled on the ribbon she’d been curling. “Evanora.” Her voice was wary, measured. “What a surprise.”

What wasn’t a surprise, however, was the smirk that made its way onto Evanora’s face upon realising that Rio already knew who she was. Evanora wondered if the younger woman was intimidated by her. It’d surely make her visit easier.

“It’s about time,” she said crisply, closing the door behind her with a snap that sounded louder than necessary. Like she was accustomed to taking up more space than she deserved. “I’ve been meaning to pay you a visit.”

“Me?” Rio placed the bouquet aside and met her gaze head-on. “How sweet.” The sarcasm dripped from her voice. “But you really shouldn’t have.”

Evanora stepped closer, eyes raking over the shop’s soft hues and blooming life like it all personally offended her somehow. “You’ve been spending a lot of time with Agatha. And with my grandson.”

Rio winced internally. That woman didn’t deserve to call Nicky her grandson.

There it was. No pleasantries—nothing Evanora said would ever be pleasant anyway. Just the barb at the heart of the visit.

“I have,” Rio responded, simply.

The older woman’s lips curled. “I didn’t see you as the type that would encourage her to keep Nicholas from seeing his father. Don’t think I don’t know about the little trip you had,” Evanora all but spat in her face, although Rio wouldn’t put it past her if she’d been standing any closer.

“If you truly cared about them, you’d leave Agatha alone.”

Rio blinked, then let out a short, incredulous laugh. “Why? I’ve treated her with nothing but respect. That’s more than either of you can say.”

Evanora’s eyes narrowed, but she didn’t flinch. “Respect?” she echoed, voice sharp and shaking slightly with restrained venom. “That woman doesn’t deserve respect. She was born wrong, Rio. You think you’re doing good by sticking around? You’re feeding the rot. She’s always been sick, it’s in her. Anyone with eyes can see it.”

The florist’s breath caught in her chest. The words didn’t just sting—they scorched. Her hands clenched at her sides, small crescent moons forming in the palm of her hand. “You don’t know what you’re talking about,” she said, voice low. Controlled. “Agatha is kind. She’s good. And she’s raised Nicky with more love than—”

“—Than a sinner like her should’ve ever been allowed to,” Evanora snapped. Her voice was rising now, gaining a feverish pitch. “And now she’s wrapped up in you, dragging a child into the middle of your disgusting—”

“Enough.”

Rio’s voice sliced clean through the room like a knife through silk. She had to fight the urge to slam her fist on the counter, instead opting to breathe deeply as she navigated the tense situation. “You don’t get to come in here and say things like that. I won’t let you.”

With flared nostrils, Evanora continued. “I’m warning you, Rio. You’re a danger. To Agatha, To Nicholas. I’ve heard what you did to his father—”

The younger woman stepped around the counter, standing taller now, meeting her fully. “And I’d do it again,” she said, unapologetic. “Father,” Rio rolled her eyes and scoffed. “Men like him don’t deserve that title. Besides, I’ll do whatever is necessary if it means keeping the people I care about safe from people like you.”

Evanora tilted her head. “Is that supposed to scare me?”

“It’s supposed to tell you that I won’t let you tear Agatha down again.” Her voice was steel now, even if her heart beat like thunder in her chest. Her mind flashed with the memory of Agatha and how broken she looked when Rio had found her at her house. She had looked like a fragile little girl, wounded by the words of someone who was supposed to be a parental figure in her life. “So maybe it’s time for you to go.”

Rio watched the woman closely and saw the way she smirked, with the tightness of someone who never smiled with warmth. “What are you going to do?” Evanora sneered. “Hit me like you hit Ralph?

“Maybe.” Rio’s grin turned sharp and slow. “Wanna come find out?”

For a heartbeat, neither of them moved. Brown eyes locked on blue, but it was nothing like her favourite pair. If eyes were the window to one’s soul, Rio was convinced Evanora didn’t have one.

Then the older woman scoffed, disgusted, and turned sharply on her heel. “Don’t say I didn’t warn you about her.” The words were thrown like a curse behind her as the bell jingled again and the door slammed shut in her wake.

Rio exhaled. Her knuckles ached from how hard she’d clenched her fists. At least that was better than the ache she’d feel if she had hit the woman.

She turned back to the counter, leaning against it, the bouquet still sitting there like a small anchor in a shaken world.

Picking up her phone, she saw the notification on her screen from earlier that day. A little status update from Agatha. It had made her smile when she received it, and it made her smile now. Rio let herself unclench her jaw and went back to curling ribbons carefully, precisely, like grounding herself in soft velvety things could hold back all the poison that had just walked through her door.

—————

It was later that night, after Agatha had finished putting away the groceries that she’d gotten with Nicky, that she felt the buzz of a notification in her phone.

Rio: im so over this week already

Along with the message came a picture. Agatha had to squint to understand what she was looking at. There was green glass scattered all over the floor, along with the water and flowers that had been sitting in it previously.

Without any hesitation, Agatha clicked the call button on their chat. After only one ring, Rio picked up.

“Hi, honey,” Agatha breathed into the phone, a hint of a smile in her voice. She could almost hear the relief in Rio’s voice when she responded with a soft, “Hi.”

In the midst of her own hectic start of the week, Agatha realised she hadn’t taken the time to check in with Rio. She had thought about the florist a lot, so much so that she and Nicky now had new plans for their free evening the next day, but she hadn’t seen or spoken to her. “Talk to me, what’s going on?”

“It’s just been a lot,” Rio started hesitantly, not really sure what exactly to give away.

Agatha nodded patiently, even if Rio couldn’t see it. She took a seat on the couch, tucking her legs under her body. “Wanna tell me what happened?”

“Well, first the AI guy from yesterday, then Evanora fucked with my head.” Hold on, what? Agatha questioned internally whether she had heard her correctly. Did she say Evanora? “and now I knocked over my favourite vase…”

“Wait, what?” was all that Agatha could push out.

“Yeah, there’s shards of glass everywhere. I had to take Luci hostage to make sure he didn’t get hurt. I think he might hate me,” Rio rambled on, words spilling out of her mouth at an impossible speed.

Agatha blinked, confused. “No, I mean, Evanora? What did she want?”

“Oh, that.” Rio realised her mistake. She didn’t plan on telling Agatha that way. She had formed all these sentences in her head that would perfectly explain what happened, but now they were nowhere to be found.

“Yeah?” Agatha made sure to keep her voice soft and gentle, despite her confusion and the anxiety bubbling inside of her. She wasn’t mad at Rio, she merely wanted to understand.

“She basically told me you’re not good for me—” “That motherfucker—” Agatha cursed under her breath, but Rio didn’t register it well enough to let herself be interrupted. “—and that I should stay away from you.”

There was a short beat of silence. Agatha tried to picture the two of them in Rio’s shop, arguing about whether or not Agatha deserved to be loved. Or better yet, whether she deserved to be alive. Perfectly normal conversation to have on a random Tuesday. Certainly nothing new for Agatha.

She was almost afraid to ask, not sure she wanted to hear the answer. What if her mother ruined the second-best thing she had going for her right now? “W-What did you say to her?”

Agatha could hear shuffling from the other end of the line, and she could feel Rio’s hesitation. The older woman braced herself for the response. “I might have… threatened her?”

Oh?

That wasn’t exactly what Agatha had expected. But then again, this was the same Rio that had punched Ralph in the face without any hesitation or remorse.

Agatha’s lack of verbal response must’ve worried Rio. “I mean, sort of. I’m sorry. I know it wasn’t my place.”

“Rio…” The florist wasn’t sure what to make of Agatha’s tone. Was it disbelief? Admiration? Shock?

“I’m sorry, okay? I just couldn’t let her say those things about you… About Nicky…”

With her heart beating wildly in her ribcage, the contents threatening to spill out, Agatha quickly reassured her. “You have nothing to apologise for. Thank you for standing up for me, again.”

“I wish I didn’t have to, not like this…” Rio said softly.

There was a pained smile on Agatha’s face. She wished she could’ve protected Rio from all of this. All of her mess. But then she wouldn’t get to see this protective side of Rio, and selfishly, Agatha wouldn’t want to miss out.

“Is there something horribly wrong with me if I told you that I find this really hot?”

“Agatha!” Rio mock-scolded through the phone. The older woman knew Rio well enough to know that she was smiling hard when she spoke her next words. “You’re crazy.”

“We already established that a long time ago, sweetheart.”

The rest of their phone call turned to lighter topics, and they stayed on the phone until both of their faces hurt from smiling, and their eyes burned from fatigue. Talking to each other made the storm inside Rio calm a little, and only made Agatha more certain of her plan for the following day.

When the sun came up the next morning, Agatha’s routine went as usual, but there was an additional factor contributing to her being in a particularly good mood. Instead of being eager to get Nicky to school and settle into work, she was more excited to pick him up again, even if it meant waltzing through the lifeless hallways of his school.

She waited in front of his classroom for his release, wrapping him up in a tight hug before he started rambling about his day, sharing all the new things he learned and who fought with whom. Agatha had a small chat with Mrs. Davis before collecting Nicky’s belongings and walking them back to the car. They quickly swung by the cafe for a folding box, then drove home.

As soon as Agatha unlocked their front door, Nicky bounded inside ahead of his mother, his little backpack bouncing with each step.

“Cupcake time! Cupcake time!” he chanted, dropping his bag near the kitchen counter, shoes kicked off along the way. Agatha stepped in after him, slipping off her boots with a soft laugh.

“Hang on, little baker,” she teased, tossing her keys on the kitchen counter. “We’ve still got to wash our hands and get everything set up.”

But Nicky had been long gone, escaped into the pantry, and collected the ingredients that they had bought the previous day, the contents threatening to spill from his arms as he carried two large bags of flour. Agatha prayed it wouldn’t rip, because she couldn’t deal with that clean-up.

Full of excitement, he set down the ingredients, trying to recount if he had missed anything. Agatha knew that he had, but didn’t say anything just yet. She ruffled his hair and pointed toward the sink. “Hands first.”

As they both washed up, the clatter of utensils and the scent of vanilla extract—because Rio liked vanilla syrup, so she must like vanilla extract, Agatha reasoned—quickly filled the space. Agatha moved with practised ease, pulling out the flour, sugar, butter and eggs, while Nicky stood on his step stool beside her, already whisk in hand like a mini sous-chef.

Today wasn’t just about baking. Not the way they usually spent their free afternoons.

Today, Agatha had a plan.

She glanced down at the small, messy notes she’d scribbled on a small post-it, which doubled as her grocery list: vanilla cupcakes, floral icing, maybe sprinkles? → spell it out. subtle. not too cheesy is cheesy ok???

“I’m ready!” Nicky declared, holding up a cracked egg, already proud of himself for not getting any shell in the bowl. It seemed like their practice had paid off.

Agatha smiled, her heart full. “Perfect. We need six more of those.”

An hour later, the heat of the oven was slowly warming up the kitchen, while Nicky crouched in front of the glass door, watching the cupcakes puff and rise like little golden clouds. His mother stood behind him, arms crossed, a smear of flower across her apron that was slightly askew.

“So…” she said gently, crouching beside him. “You remember what we talked about earlier?”

Nicky grinned, eyes still fixed on the cupcakes. “I can’t tell Rio. Yet.” He turned, conspiratorially. “Even if I really want to.”

She let out a short laugh. “That’s right, baby. You’ve got this. We just have to decorate them first.”

Together they prepared the frosting, grabbing various small bowls and adding food colouring to each one. Agatha filled the piping bags and instructed Nicky not to squeeze it to death as soon as he got his hands on one. The boy hummed as he concentrated, tongue peeking out from the corner of his mouth, while Agatha took her time with each cupcake. Some were delicate, others rather chaotic, but each one was unmistakably made with love. Agatha pretended not to notice when two mysteriously vanished, accepting that they’d been “taste tested” by the little chef for quality control.

When Agatha finished arranging them in the way she liked, she sealed the box with a short, soft sigh.

“I think she’ll love it!” Nicky chimed in, a proud grin on his face. To which Agatha responded, nervously, “I hope so.”

It was adorable that he tried to reassure his mother. Agatha felt both proud and lucky to have such a thoughtful son.

“And if she doesn’t, I’ll eat them all myself!”

Of course, he remained a child, and when there were cupcakes at stake, he’d always have his eyes on the prize.

She glanced down at their heartfelt creation again, heart beating faster now. It was a little messy. But it was theirs. Just like the rest of this strange, wonderful thing they’d been building.

Before she could talk herself out of it, Agatha discarded her apron, quickly checking her reflection in the mirror to make sure she looked okay. She wore a beige knit vest over a white linen shirt and brown loose-fitting pants. Now that she had wiped off the remaining flour stains, she looked very well put together. She flipped her body over, tousling her hair at the roots before throwing it back again, hoping that it’d give her some extra volume.

After applying her clear lip gloss—she didn’t want to make it seem like she was trying very hard—she nodded at the reflection once, satisfied.

Moments later, she had dropped Nicky off at the cafe and found herself walking over to the flower shop across the street. Her pulse was strong, as if her heart was sitting in her throat and having its own party. Nothing to be celebrated (yet?).

She peered inside and saw Rio with her back facing the door, caught in the tranquil rhythm of her work, humming along to the music softly under her breath. Agatha stood just inside the threshold for a moment, admiring her, one hand hidden behind her back holding a white plastic bag with the cake box, the other gripping a to-go coffee cup still steaming at the rim.

She figured now was as good a time as ever, as she didn’t think her nerves would ebb away any time soon.

“Special delivery,” Agatha said finally, her voice warm and teasing but hiding a tremor as she announced her presence.

Rio’s head snapped up and back in her direction, surprise blooming across her face when she saw Agatha. Her eyes lit up—Agatha could tell even from afar—and her brows lifted as she set her shears down and removed her apron. The action revealed her outfit, and for a moment, Agatha forgot what she came over for. The florist wore dark jeans and a black and white checkered button-up with short sleeves, but only the top button was fastened. Underneath the shirt sat a black top, just a tiny bit too short to cover the entirety of her stomach. Agatha didn’t mind. The older woman watched in awe as Rio moved towards the side of the counter, slightly leaning against the surface.

“Well, well.” A soft smile played on Rio’s lips. “It’s been a while since I’ve had one of those.”

Agatha held out the coffee cup with a smirk, but her eyes were soft as they flicked over Rio’s face, as though quietly taking inventory of how tired she might be, how many bouquets she had arranged today, whether she’d eaten lunch. “That’s why I made it extra special this time.”

Rio accepted it with a grateful sigh, fingers brushing Agatha’s for a second too long as she took the cup. Even the smallest touch sent shivers down her spine. She took a sip, using the moment as an opportunity to take in Agatha’s appearance. As always, Rio couldn’t help but admire the older woman. “You spoil me.”

“Handmade by yours truly. With the help of your favourite Harkness.” Agatha said with a tilt of her head.

Feigning innocence, Rio raised an eyebrow, sipping the drink again. “You mean you?”

Agatha gave her a mock-offended look. “Please. We don’t have to pretend. Nicky wins by a landslide.”

Rio laughed. “I plead the Fifth.”

Though at the mention of Nicky, Rio looked toward the front of the shop as if expecting to see him come tumbling in behind Agatha. “Wait, where is he?” she asked. “You didn’t bring him?”

The older woman hesitated, fingers tightening slightly around the bag still hidden behind her back. “He’s back at the cafe with Lilia. You can come by later if you like.”

Rio cocked her head, confusion flickering across her features. Why hadn’t Agatha just brought him along like she usually would? Perhaps things were worse than Rio thought.

As if reading her mind, Agatha drew in a breath, suddenly uncertain of how to keep the words straight in her head when her heart was thudding like a drum in her chest. “I thought… maybe we should talk,” she started carefully. “If that’s okay. If now’s not a good time, I can come back later—”

Rio’s spine went rigid. Her heartbeat kicked up sharply, doing a strange little lurch in her chest. Her grip on the coffee cup adjusted, just slightly. The last time someone said, we need to talk, it hadn’t ended well. It rarely ever did. Talk. That was never just a casual word when you cared about someone as much as she did about Agatha.

But she swallowed the instinct to deflect. It got caught in her throat, fighting its way back up, but Rio persevered, swallowing once again.

Instead, she met Agatha’s gaze. She hoped the cafe owner wouldn’t be able to read the panic in her eyes. “No, no. Now’s fine. Is everything okay?”

“Yeah,” Agatha nodded, but her expression was serious. “I just wanted to talk about us, after, you know… without any distractions.”

There was a beat of silence.

There was a panic slowly threading up Rio’s spine, and her chest tightened. Us. The word filled the air between them, sweet and heavy like overripe fruit.

She blinked a few times and set down her coffee while she tried to muster something easy, dismissive, self-protective. She’d been here before. “Look, Agatha,” she ran her hand through her hair, out of nervous habit, and caught how Agatha’s eyes tracked the movement. “We can just forget about it if you regret—”

But Agatha was already moving, effectively cutting off Rio’s sentence.

The older woman stepped closer, gently setting the bag on the counter. Carefully, she took out the box that she’d hidden inside. Rio watched curiously, her mouth still slightly ajar from the interruption.

When Agatha lifted the lid, she revealed a dozen cupcakes inside. Some were elegantly piped, others clearly decorated by tiny, enthusiastic hands, frosting smudged in places, and pastel flowers a little lopsided. But the intention was clear; they had decorated their cupcakes with various flowers, finished off with green leaves. They were beautiful in their imperfection.

On a few, in slightly wonky but unmistakable letters, a dark-green frosting spelled out:

B E M I N E ?

Rio stared, heart beating so loudly she could barely hear anything else. “You… made these?”

Agatha nodded. Her voice was softer now, but steady. “With Nicky. But this part—” she pointed to the message. “This was all me.”

The florist looked up from the box, eyes wide, lips parted in shock. She felt the urge to pinch herself to make sure she wasn’t dreaming. Had she accidentally stocked flowers that caused hallucinations?

Agatha drew in a breath, letting go of whatever fear she’d been clinging to. The conversations with Wanda and Nicky replayed in her head, their words of advice echoing on loop. Time to make Nicky proud. And to make herself proud.

The realisation hit her only then, registering the words Rio had spoken mere minutes ago. She seemed genuinely worried, worried that Agatha had wanted to take back what had happened. That never crossed her mind. She finally understood what Wanda meant when she told Agatha that Rio might need some words of affirmation.

Agatha didn’t exactly know how. This wasn’t her area of expertise. But for Rio, she was willing to try.

She let the silence stretch for another beat, then looked up at Rio with something raw and vulnerable in her eyes. She gave a soft, almost embarrassed laugh.

“Darling,” she began gently, trying to keep her voice steady, “I’ve been trying to do everything I can to show you that I definitely didn’t regret that night. And I definitely want it to happen again.”

Rio blinked. Her throat was still tight, but she felt the tension in her shoulders ease ever so slightly. Her eyes flicked from the cupcakes to Agatha’s face, like she was trying to recalibrate.

Agatha pressed on, voice warming as she spoke. Blue eyes met brown with the utmost sincerity in them. “You’ve managed to tear me open in a way I didn’t expect, and I realised, I’m not that eager to sew myself shut again.” Agatha looked down, fiddling with the corner of the cake box for a moment before looking back up, doubling down on her statement. “Not if it’s you I’m opening up to.”

The florist’s breath hitched, feeling caught between wanting to laugh, cry or kiss her all at once. Her chest filled with something impossibly light. Something she hadn’t dared to name until now.

She tried to say something, but the words lodged in her throat.

So, Agatha smiled through her nerves and reached for Rio’s hand, lacing their fingers together while she prepared to say the words she hadn’t let herself say before.

“Rio, will you be my girlfriend?”

Agatha was afraid to blink, afraid to miss even a second of the reaction. Rio’s lips curled before her mind could even catch up. Her smile spread, slow and stunned, the kind of expression people get when something beautiful blindsides them in the best way.

“Yes,” she said, almost laughing through the disbelief. “Yes. Of course.” She tightened the hold on Agatha’s hand, giving a slight squeeze.

The cafe owner’s shoulders relaxed with a kind of quiet relief, her grin cracking open like sunlight through cloud cover.

Finally, she did what she’d been aching to do. She stepped toward Rio, capturing the florist between the counter and her body, and closed the distance, kissing her. Lightly at first, then deeper, savouring the newfound feeling of kissing one’s girlfriend. While Rio’s hands settled on the small of Agatha’s back, the older woman's free hand found its way to Rio’s waist, slipping just under the hem of the button-up shirt, thumb caressing the small sliver of stomach that wasn’t covered by her top. Both of them smiled mid-kiss like they couldn’t believe it was actually happening.

When they broke the kiss, Rio brushed their noses together. “So,” she started, placing another quick peck on Agatha’s lips, just because she could. She bit her lip, and there was a hint of teasing in her voice when she spoke. “Can I go see my favourite Harkness now?”

“Oh, fuck you.” Agatha leaned back and rolled her eyes in mock annoyance.

Rio slipped out of her hold, stepping away from the counter. “Later, baby. That’s next on the priority list.”

Their laughter filled the store as they made their way outside, Agatha following closely behind Rio. Agatha’s cheeks were flushed, her expression caught somewhere between bashful and proud. The younger woman looked calmer than she felt, as her heart was practically beating out in Morse code under her shirt: Agatha Harkness is my girlfriend now.

They stepped inside the cafe, the scent of cinnamon and freshly ground coffee beans welcoming them in like old friends. Like nothing had changed.

Before Agatha could even hear the door close behind her, a blur of motion shot from behind the counter.

“Nicky!” Lilia called, but too late.

He was already racing, eyes wide with anticipation. He stopped in front of them and looked up, practically vibrating.

“Andandand?” he asked, eyes bouncing between them. “Did it work?”

Agatha let out a soft snort, and Rio looked at her sideways, grinning like she’d just won a grand prize. In her eyes, she had.

“Way to be subtle,” Agatha muttered at Nicky’s overly excited behaviour. She was lucky it had all turned out okay.

Rio ignored Agatha’s comment and crouched to meet him at eye level. “It did work, buddy.”

Agatha crossed her arms with a mock sigh, the corners of her mouth pulling up into a subtle smile. “You’re looking at two very official girlfriends.”

“Really?!” Nicky gasped and looked positively starstruck.

“Yes, really,” Rio confirmed, and the little boy immediately threw both arms around her neck, hugging her with force. Agatha looked down at them, her chest softening in that way it only did when she watched the two of them together.

But Nicky, ever the opportunist, pulled back and tilted his head, looking at his mother.

“So..” he said, drawing the word out with calculated innocence. “Does that mean I don’t get to eat alllll the cupcakes?”

“I thought you’d be happy for us!”

“I am, Mama! But I’d also be happy if I could eat another one.”

Neither woman had ever been able to deny him of anything, especially something like this, and it’s almost as if he knew exactly which heartstrings to pull at to play them like his own little puppets.

They had already anticipated that, though, so Rio held out her hand in Agatha’s direction, who placed a single cupcake into her hand. Carefully piped in icing on top was the capital N. Used for a very important question, and the initial of the name of a very important child.

“I had a feeling you’d ask,” Rio laughed softly as she held it out. “I think you’ve earned it. Maybe even two.”

Nicky took the cupcake like it was made of gold. Like he hadn’t sneakily eaten one earlier that afternoon. As he skipped off, cupcake clutched in both hands to show Lilia, Rio stood up again, and Agatha glanced at her, exhaling a laugh.

“You’re really good with him,” she complimented, a little awed.

Rio nudged her shoulder gently, smirking. “I’m good with both of you.”

Chapter End Notes

Chapter 20: the ordinary feels extraordinary

Chapter Notes

well well well. look who found her way back to this.

apologies, life was crazy.
not in the “i was stuck in a coma” author’s note kind of crazy, but in the way that i got semi burned out at work, finally came out to my ethnic family, relationship unexpectedly ended a few days before my best friend’s wedding, had a few will-they-won’t-they and should-we-shouldn’t-we with said ex, before rolling into hot girl summer & pride szn. Safe to say writing was not the first thing on my mind :P

anyway, enjoy!

See the end of the chapter for more notes

The next couple of days passed in a blur. A rose-colored, filled-with-kisses-and-cuddles-and-family-dinners-after-a-long-day-of-work type of blur. The two women had been working in tandem, checking in with one another whenever they could, and spending a significant amount of their free time together. Not that they had much free time, but at least they were both understanding, as the two business owners were in similar positions.

There was something strange about how easily they had slipped into it. Not the relationship itself. No, that was still new enough to make Rio occasionally pause when she caught herself doing something that would have been unthinkable only a few weeks ago.

Like waking up in Agatha’s arms, and tucking a strand of hair behind the older woman’s ear before wishing her a good morning. Or sitting on the couch with Nicky, watching cartoons before dinner was ready.

It was the togetherness that felt surprisingly normal.

Rio had started appearing at the cafe in the late afternoon, sometimes to keep up with her caffeine addiction, sometimes to show off a new flower that she got in stock. Sometimes with nothing but an exhausted smile and a, “Do you have five minutes?” just because she needed to rant, or simply missed being close to Agatha.

Agatha always seemed to find five. Sometimes five turned into fifteen. Sometimes five turned into the older woman taking off her apron and telling Billy and Lilia to close up without her, so that she could take her girlfriend home.

There were texts throughout the day, too. Agatha had never been the type to be glued to her phone, but she was enjoying the small moments of connection with her partner throughout the day.

Rio: have you eaten?

No.

Rio: Agatha.

It’s been busy.

Rio: that wasn’t the question

And although Rio knew that there was plenty of food available at the cafe for Agatha to eat, she also knew her partner would be too distracted to take care of herself properly. On the days that Lilia was around, she trusted that Agatha would be well-fed. Still, that didn’t stop Rio from bringing over a nice meal, or walking into the cafe with the excuse of “needing coffee and a snack” so that Agatha would join her.

Agatha made sure to return the favour, too.

How’s your day?

Rio: terrible

Want to come over tonight? I’ll make your favourite.

Rio: you’re the best

Anything for you, dear.

Agatha had spent so long convincing herself that getting too close to Rio would complicate everything, that she hadn’t considered how uncomplicated it might feel once they stopped fighting it. It was embarrassingly easy.

There were no grand declarations. No dramatic promises about forever. Though they both had been going through that mental exercise in their own way, toying with the idea of a future, together. While they had become better at communicating, certain things still felt like a step too far. They hadn’t crossed the particular threshold of saying the words that seemed to carry so much weight. No matter how good things felt, the idea of a “forever” still seemed slightly too frightening.

Instead, there were little things. The little things were easy.

Rio’s hand finding hers beneath the table during dinner. Agatha, in return, caressing the back of her hand gently.

Agatha leaning against the younger woman’s shoulder while Nicky talked animatedly about something that had happened at school.

Rio remembering exactly how Nicky liked his school lunch to be packed, effortlessly taking over whenever she had spent the night.

Agatha keeping one of Rio’s hair ties around her wrist because Rio had forgotten it at her place and apparently couldn’t be trusted to remember to take things home. But there were certain things the barista suspected Rio left on purpose, just to make her stays at the Harkness house easier.

Unsurprisingly, Nicky adapted to the changes faster than either of them. He didn’t appear to be particularly interested in the complicated emotional history that had preceded it. As far as he was concerned, Rio was simply Rio, and Rio was around more often now. And his mother seemed to enjoy that just as much as he did, if not more.

Agatha had found herself watching the pair from the kitchen more than once, smiling to herself while Rio and Nicky were playing his newest addition to the collection, Gigantosaurus the Game. It was a gift from Rio, but Agatha was not sure whether she got the game for Nicky or herself.

There were moments like those, when she felt something settle quietly in her chest. Something still unfamiliar, but a feeling eager to make itself home in Agatha’s body and mind.

Not fear, not exactly. Something warmer. Something that a made her wonder, if only for a second, what it would be like if this became normal.

If Rio being in her living room became something she stopped noticing, because she expected it. If Nicky stopped thinking of her visits as visits at all. If one day there was no distinction between Rio’s place and theirs. Between Rio’s life and theirs.

The thought usually appeared and went before Agatha could examine it too closely. She had learned, recently, that perhaps not every feeling needed to be dissected and acted upon the moment it appeared.

Some things could simply exist.

Besides, Agatha had enough on her mind. Work had been relentless. The cafe was busy. Which was a good thing, she knew that. Times had been different in the past. But that also meant that there were suppliers to chase, invoices to check, orders to place, and approximately twelve things that needed fixing at any given moment.

And lately, Agatha had been preoccupied by something else, too. Someone else.

She wasn’t entirely sure when she had started noticing her.

It wasn’t as though there had been one particular moment when Agatha had suddenly decided she cared. It had happened gradually, almost imperceptibly, until she’d realised she was paying attention.

She noticed when the girl arrived. When she left. Where she sat. What she ordered. How long she stayed. How she contemplated her words before speaking them aloud.

And lately, the cafe owner had started thinking about what she might like.

That was how Agatha ended up standing in the middle of the cafe at eight in the morning, staring at a table.

She moved it three inches to the left. Stopped. Tilted her head. Moved it back.

“No,” she sighed. It looked wrong.

She walked around it, imagining the space from another perspective. Still wrong. Agatha grabbed the edge and dragged it toward the wall.

Better.

She stepped back, crossing her arms and biting the inside of her cheek.

Maybe.

She was still staring at it when Lilia walked through the door. “Good morning,” the older woman said.

Agatha didn’t answer immediately, so Lilia just kept looking at her while she set her bag down. “Agatha.”

“Hm?”

“What are you doing?” The suspicion dripped from her tone.

“Thinking.” As if that was not obvious, Agatha added in her head.

“About?”

“The layout. In here. Or whatever.”

Lilia looked around the cafe. Then at Agatha. Then at the table. She couldn’t remember the last time any major changes were done.

“The layout,” Lilia repeated, like it would suddenly start making sense.

“Yes.”

“At… eight in the morning?”

Agatha finally looked at her. “Do you have somewhere else you’d rather be?”

Lilia smiled. She was used to Agatha’s behaviour by now, even if she seemed extra defensive this morning. “No.”

“Then I suggest you mind your own business.” Agatha turned back toward the table.

“Of course,” Lilia replied, watching her for another few seconds while tying her apron before grabbing baking supplies from the back room. She knew better than to ask.

The next day, Agatha decided to change the lighting.

She stood beneath the ceiling fixture, looking up with a frown. Too bright.

After she replaced the bulb, it was still too bright. So, she replaced it again. Better.

Then, she stood in the middle of the cafe and looked toward the back corner and nodded to herself. “Much better.”

“Talking to yourself again?” a voice sounded. Agatha nearly dropped the box of light bulbs, startled. She turned to find Billy standing in the doorway, grinning way too brightly for her liking.

“I wasn’t talking to myself,” she lamely defended. “I was thinking.”

“Out loud,” he challenged. Agatha hated how comfortable the teen had gotten since the cafe owner had decided to let an ounce of emotion slip through. Just because her cold heart had melted a little because of the florist, didn’t mean he was suddenly safe.

He looked at her, then at the newly dimmed light. Then right back at her. Agatha narrowed her eyes. “If you have something to say—“

“I don’t.” Billy made a gesture with his hands, pretending to zip his lips and throw away the key.

“Good.” Agatha’s shoulders relaxed slightly.

By the end of the week, the cafe had begun to change in small, almost imperceptible ways; one table had disappeared. Another, slightly larger one, had been moved. The large wooden bookshelf that had once been near the front window was now against the back wall.

Agatha had sorted through the books and went to the local bookshop down the street, grabbing copies of her own favourites to add to the collection. She also made a mental note to go through her books at home, deciding that some of them deserved a new home.

She didn’t tell anyone what had spurred on these changes. She didn’t have to. While Agatha was hard to read, it wasn’t impossible. Especially for the ones closest to her.

Sometime that week, during Rio’s usual coffee run in the afternoon, the younger woman decided to poke around a little. She was sitting on one of the stools at the counter, watching how Agatha poured her coffee order just right.

“Are you redecorating?” Rio asked, head tilting to the back of the cafe.

Agatha didn’t look up, skilfully pouring her latte art into the cup. “No.”

Rio smiled with a hint of mischief. “Hmm, let’s see. You’re moving furniture.”

“Yes.” She handed Rio her cappuccino, after adding a splash of vanilla.

“Buying new lamps.”

“Mhm.”

“New books,” Rio added on. She wrapped her hands around the warm cup, a smile making its way to her lips before taking a careful sip.

“Yes, baby. Thank you for the observations.”

Rio tilted her head. Agatha found it adorable. “But you’re not redecorating?”

“No,” Agatha insisted nonchalantly. “Is there a point to this interrogation, officer?”

The florist flushed slightly at the mental imagery that that brought back, poking her tongue into the inside of her cheek before shaking her head to rid herself of those thoughts. Now was not the time to think about their latest experiment in the bedroom. Very successful experiment, Rio would argue. “I’m just curious.”

“I can tell.” Agatha grabbed a piece of banana bread from the display, breaking a small piece off the corner before handing the rest to her girlfriend. She popped the piece in her mouth and lightly sucked her fingers clean in the process.

Rio happily accepted the treat that was offered to her, and took a bite of her own. “So, should I be worried about who you’re trying to impress in here?”

There was amusement in Rio’s voice, but something else underneath it. Not jealousy, exactly. Curiosity. A little worry, maybe.

Agatha studied her face, then smiled gently. “Not at all, sweetheart.”

The younger woman’s expression softened, only perceptible to the attentive eye. It was minimal, only noticeable in the way her eyebrows seemed to relax ever so slightly. “Okay,” she said with a nod.

The cafe owner reached across the counter and took Rio’s hand and squeezed it. Rio smiled. She’d never not react to the feeling of Agatha holding her hand, no matter how often they did that now.

“Just know that you can ask me for help whenever. You know that right?”

Agatha stepped around the counter, coming up to Rio’s side. She cupped her girlfriend’s cheek and tilted her face upwards, planting a soft kiss on her lips. “Thank you, honey. I’ll keep that in mind.”

The next afternoon, when Rio yet again needed her caffeine shot, she walked in searching for her favourite pair of blue eyes. However, she was met with Lilia’s gentle greeting. “Hi, darling.”

“Hi, Lilia, whatcha making?” Rio sniffed the air, the scent of freshly baked goods wrapping her into a warm hug.

“Here, let me know what you think.” It was a puff pastry filled with apple and cinnamon, the inside still steaming hot when Rio took a bite.

The florist covered her mouth with her hand while she attempted the treat to cool down in her mouth. “Hot,” she puffed. “But so good,” she added.

Lilia let out a soft laugh, and Billy came up behind the counter, grabbing the piston to grind the coffee beans into without being told what Rio’s order was. From one day to the other, suddenly everyone in there knew exactly how she liked her coffee.

“Where’s Agatha?” Rio asked once she was all caught up with the other two.

“I don’t know,” Billy shrugged, “She left a while ago to pick something up. She should be back soon.”

Right on cue, the bell above the door chimed, and Agatha walked in, albeit a bit bewildered. Her face lit up as soon as she saw Rio inside. “Perfect, you’re here!”

“Yes, babe. I’ve been right here.” Had Agatha been looking for her? “Where were you?”

Agatha had found an old armchair in a second-hand furniture shop and somehow managed to convince herself that she absolutely needed it. It was worn, soft, and slightly too large for the space. It barely fit into her car, but she couldn’t leave it behind.

Foregoing any small talk, Agatha grabbed her hand and dragged Rio outside, towards the car that was parked halfway on the sidewalk. She opened the door, looking at Rio teasingly. “If only my strong, muscular girlfriend could help me out.”

Rio chuckled softly and ignored the way she could feel her cheeks heat up, immediately stepping in to help. She rolled up her sleeves till they sat right above her elbows, and gently moved Agatha out of the way so she could reach the chair properly.

Rio let out a puff of air, lifting the chair out of the car and adjusting her grip so she could carry it independently, even though Agatha stepped in to lend her a hand. “Just open the door for me, princess. I’ve got it.”

Too stunned by the nickname to argue, Agatha opened the door for the florist to step through, making sure she managed carrying the piece of furniture alone. Instead of setting it down immediately upon entering the cafe, Rio instinctively walked to the back corner, setting the chair down right next to the bookshelf. “All good here?”

“Perfect,” Agatha smiled. She turned back around to her car, retrieving a few more items from her trunk.

Rio walked back to the counter, where Billy and Lilia were whispering quietly. “It’s funny how she won’t admit it, but she really cares.”

“Hm?” Rio questioned, not yet catching on.

“That corner,” Lilia tilted her head, drying one of the coffee mugs with a towel, “she’s been so busy with it.”

“Yeah, I could tell. It looks nice, doesn’t it?”

“It does. Let’s hope she feels the same.”

“Who is this for, exactly?”

“She didn’t tell you?” Billy asked.

The florist shook her head. She hadn’t pressed Agatha for more information. She simply let her girlfriend be. She figured she’d come to her eventually.

Agatha walked back in, right past them as if they weren’t there, and added a small round side table beside the chair. She carefully unwrapped a lamp from its bubblewrap.

Rio looked over to the newly changed corner. There was a stack of notebooks in the bookshelf, seemingly new. A few novels. Pencils in a little ceramic cup.

A small sign sat on the bookshelf. ‘TAKE A BOOK. LEAVE A BOOK.’ scribbled in Agatha’s handwriting.

“It’s for Hannah,” Billy continued. When Rio looked at him with a hint of confusion, he continued, “You know, the girl who always comes in to study here.”

Rio’s expression changed. “The girl with the blue backpack? With all the pins on it?”

Billy nodded, and Rio fell quiet for a moment while she watched Agatha add a purple cushion to the seat, fluffing it up a bit.

Lilia leaned against the counter, a proud, motherly smile on her face. “Apparently it’s hard for her to study at home,” she commented about the teen. “Something about it being too loud.”

The florist’s eyes softened, looking over at Agatha once again. “She told Agatha that?”

“Once,” Lilia replied.

“And Agatha remembered.” Rio smiled to herself. “Of course she did.”

Rio looked around again.

The table. Slightly bigger. Not big enough for a group, but large enough to fit notebooks and a laptop. Positioned further from the loud coffee machine.

The lamps. The one on the ceiling slightly dimmed, warmer, more gentle on the eyes. The one on the side table bright enough to read on cloudy days.

The books, offered to anyone who might feel encouraged to read more.

The blankets, added to a basket. “Just in case people get cold,” Agatha had said.

It suddenly made sense. Every strange little change. Every time Agatha had stood in the middle of the cafe staring at a chair. Every time she’d complained about the lighting. Every book she carefully picked.

Rio laughed quietly. “I can’t believe she didn’t tell me.”

“She doesn’t want people to know.” Though Lilia knew how to read her like the books in that bookshelf. Rio looked at her, and before she could ask why, Lilia gave her a look that seemed to say Have you met Agatha?

“It’s cute, though,” Billy interrupted, “because we all know what she’s doing anyway. She’s a big softie deep down.”

Realising the words that just came out, he quickly covered his mouth, stumbling over the words as he added, “But please don’t tell her I said that, she might kill me.”

“Oh, she would,” Rio teased. She looked over right on time to see Agatha add her finishing touches with a plant that Rio insisted would survive even if Agatha forgot to water it for several weeks.

“I do not forget to water my plants,” Agatha argued.

Rio raised an eyebrow. “You killed a cactus.”

“It was an aggressive cactus.”

“Cacti don’t have personalities,” Rio tried to hold back a smile.

“Well, my dearest plant expert, you didn’t meet this one.”

Rio laughed, then moved in to kiss her cheek. “Because you killed it before I got the chance to.”

Agatha decided to ignore that comment and simply accepted the kiss, as well as the un-killable plant.

There was something unexpectedly tender about the little reading nook. Agatha had noticed something most people would have ignored. She’d remembered. And then she’d quietly changed the space around her without ever expecting to be thanked.

She cares so much more than she lets people see, Rio thought to herself. She wondered if a younger Agatha would have needed a space like this. A place away from home that still felt like home. Safe.

Agatha’s house had never felt like a home. Not because it was too loud, but because she wasn’t allowed to be loud, at all. When she was Hannah’s age, she was careful not to set off her mother, not wanting another bomb to explode.

Rio felt her heart ache at the thought of a young Agatha, but felt a big swell of pride overpower those emotions. She couldn’t believe how far Agatha had gotten. And no matter what life threw her way, no matter how many walls she had built, there was still someone who cared for others so effortlessly. The cafe owner had shown it time and time again, but not often for strangers.

Finally satisfied with how the place looked, Agatha walked back over to her coworkers and partner. She placed a kiss on Rio’s cheek. “Thank you, babe.”

“Any time,” Rio responded wholeheartedly. She scanned Agatha’s face to make sure she was okay. When she found no sign to prove otherwise, she pulled Agatha into her side tightly, arm wrapped around her waist. This caused the older woman to look at her, and Rio was instantly taken by the gentle softness that was exuding from her favourite pair of blue eyes.

She always felt proud to be Agatha’s girlfriend, but it was days like these that really solidified her feelings.

Rio could not wait to take Agatha home today, and return the favour to show her just how much she cared. They picked Nicky up from school together, and as soon as they walked through the door, the florist ordered Agatha to sit on the couch and catch up with Nicky while she handled dinner.

By now the younger woman had familiarised herself with the various cupboards in Agatha’s kitchen, and she had grown to enjoy spending time in it. Besides, Nicky always complimented her, claiming that her cooking tasted much better than his mother’s. Rio knew it was in her best interest not to confirm nor deny.

During dinner, while Nicky had managed to get the inside of his taco spilled all over his shirt, he asked with mid-chew, “Why do you never bring Luci when you come over?”

Rio did finish chewing her food before answering. “Well, I would, but he hates it when I put him in his carrier.” She took a napkin and wiped some sauce off of his chin, and then proceeded to try to minimise the t-shirt stain before it fully formed. Agatha tried to hide her smile, but Rio caught it anyway and blushed.

“He hates it so much that he’ll probably ignore me for the rest of the day,” she added.

Taking advantage of Rio’s flustered state, Agatha decided to tease her further. “Oh, so he’s sassy like his mommy.”

Nicky laughed, taking another bite of his taco, followed by a clumsy wipe with that same, used napkin. He nodded slowly, not fully understanding but accepting the answer nonetheless.

“But, if you want to see him,” Rio looks over to Agatha, who was already looking at her with that soft smile she’d grown to love so much, “you can come visit me whenever.”

“Maybe I can sleep over, too!” he added enthusiastically, nearly bouncing in his seat from excitement.

The florist, who was still looking at Agatha, held her gaze for just a bit longer to see if the older woman seemed against the idea entirely. “If your mom says yes, of course.”

“Hmm, let me think about it…” Agatha said, tapping her chin with her forefinger and looking up at the ceiling, pretending to ignore Nicky’s chants of please please please.

“Please, Mama? You can stay too! Please!” He leaned toward Rio, wrapping his arm around her as if his mother was going to take her away from him.

“Yeah, babe, no need to be jealous. You can come, too.” Rio winked, tucking Nicky into her side and holding him tightly.

Agatha chuckled. “What makes you think I wouldn’t enjoy the opportunity of having a night to myself?”

“Whatever you say, Agatha.” Rio rolled her eyes teasingly and turned back to Nicky, looking down at two very hopeful brown eyes. She kissed the top of his head before smoothing over his hair. “What I’m hearing is that she just gave you permission. You can stay one night on the weekend, if you want?”

Usually, whenever Rio was over at their house, Nicky would keep looking for excuses to stay up longer. This time, however, he was overly eager to get into bed, wanting the weekend to come as soon as possible.

Chapter 21: Stay here, see through, find out

Chapter Summary

“I’m merely advocating for the rights of the people.” Rio held up her hands in defence.

Agatha narrowed her eyes slightly. “The people have school tomorrow, honey.”

The florist pulled Nicky into her lap, wrapping an arm around him protectively. “The people are outnumbered and being oppressed!”
